tbmm-2016-02-26sit01spe0134par0533	Mr. President, dear lawmakers, I salute your grand delegation and our esteemed citizens who are watching us on their screens. Mr. President, dear lawmakers, I would like to first share with you my assessment of the global economic outlook. The global economy has yet to enter an upward growth trend in the post-crisis period. During 2003-2007, the global economy, which averaged 5.1 per cent, averaged 3.5 per cent during the 2011-2015 period following the crisis. The global economy, which grew 3.4 per cent in 2014, is expected to slow down in 2015 and grow 3.1 per cent. This is the lowest global growth rate in the post-crisis period. In 2016, the global economy is expected to accelerate a bit and grow 3.4 per cent. Growth in developed countries has gained momentum, but has not achieved pre-crisis levels. During 2003-2007, the average of developed countries that grew by 2.8 per cent grew by an average of 1.5 per cent during 2011-2015. In the post-crisis period, the U.S. economy split in a positive direction among developed countries. The US economy, which grew 2.4% in 2014 and 2015, is estimated to grow 2.6% in 2016. However, the slowdown in the Chinese economy, low commodity prices, fragility in financial markets, uncertainty towards the future in the global economy, and poorness in the lower indicators of improved labour market with strong dollars indicate that the medium-term view of growth in the United States will be relatively moderate. With the European Central Bank's monetary enlargement policy, the eurozone is out of recession. In 2014, 0.9 per cent of the growing eurozone is expected to grow 1.5 per cent in 2015. The euro's loss of value, the decline in oil prices, the expansion of money and finance policy and the reviving of private consumption support growth in the EU. Mr. President, valued lawmakers, developing countries leading global economic growth in the pre-crisis and post-crisis period tend to slow down starting in 2014. In 2011-2013, developing countries, which contribute an average of 3.1 points to global growth, are expected to contribute an average of 2.5 points during 2014-2016. In 2014, 4.6 per cent of developing countries are expected to grow 4.3 per cent in 2015 and 4.3 per cent in 2016. The developing countries, except China and India, which grew 2.8% in 2014, are estimated to grow by 1.7 per cent in 2015 and 2.5 per cent in 2016. The uncertainty caused by the FED's monetary policy, financial vulnerability, slowdown in the Chinese economy, decline in price of oil and commodity, rising in efficiency below expected, and geopolitical problems have been affecting the growth of developing countries in a negative way, slowing them down. The Chinese economy, which grew 7.3 per cent in 2014, grew 6.9% in 2015 and is expected to grow 6.3 per cent in 2016. The Chinese economy's efforts to change its model of growth and high debt slow growth in China, limiting it. Low oil prices and geopolitical problems continue to pose risks for the Middle East and North African countries. Low commodity prices have a negative effect on Latin American countries and other commodity exporters economies. The slowdown in global demand, the FED-based uncertainty, the strengthening of the dollar and increasing geopolitical risks have been the main factors in reducing global trade volume in recent years. Global trade has only grown 3.8 per cent in the post-crisis period. Global trade volume, estimated to grow 2.6% in 2015, is expected to increase by 3.4 per cent in 2016. That's less than half of the pre-crisis average. The decline in commodity prices, particularly oil, continues to affect domestic and external macroeconomic balances in developed and developing countries. Low oil prices contribute positively to the balance of inflation and current transactions in economies with high energy dependence on energy, while oil exports affect the macroeconomic balance of developing economies. Mr. President, valued lawmakers, the expected improvement in the period following the global crisis has not been achieved, and risks related to the global economy remain, however different. I'd like to share with you the main factors that are more important than the risks involved. The future uncertainty regarding the FED's monetary policy, the slowdown of growth in China, the widespread and condensation of geopolitical tensions and condensation in the European region, the possibility of further recovery in the developing countries, the further apparent growth in low growth in developing countries, the narrowing of demand in the financial markets, the decline in financial markets and the decline in asset prices and the overall decline in debt is a barrier to global growth. Mr. President, valuable lawmakers, now I would like to share with you my brief assessment of the view of the Turkish economy: it appears that growth has continued steadily, except for 2009, when the global crisis deepened in three months view of the Turkish economy. In the post-global crisis period, between 2011-2015, Turkey grew by 4.4 per cent, while the developing countries, except China and India, grew by 3.4 per cent in the same period. In this period, Turkey has shown a growth performance of over one point in developing countries, except China and India, as well as the average growth rate. Turkey's economy grew 3.4 per cent in the first nine months of 2015 in an environment where two separate elections took place, where resuscitation in eurozone economies remained limited, and socioeconomic problems continue in our close trade partners and neighbours, where financial fluctuations and geopolitical tensions increased. As we look at 2015 as well as the first nine months of fulfillment, and as we look at the indicators, we estimate the growth rate of 2015 by 4%, and the leading indicators indicate that we will be able to capture this growth rate. It is well above the estimated 3.4 per cent for developing Europe at the same rate as 4 per cent for developing countries, 1.7 per cent for developing countries other than China and India, and 0.3 per cent for Latin America. The growth of Turkey's economy in 2015 has indeed produced a very positive result in performance. In 2015, Turkey is expected to be one of the fastest growing economies among the countries of G20, OECD and the European Union. In 2016, we expect growth to be 4.5 per cent due to economic recovery in our largest trade partner, the European Union, when political stability and reform will increase predictability. This is more than the average 4.3 per cent predicted for developing countries, 3.1 per cent for developing Europe, 2.5 per cent for developing countries other than China and India, and minus 0.3 per cent for Latin America. Mr. President, good lawmakers, inflation has taken place at 8.8 per cent on the target due to the loss of value in Turkish lira in 2015 and a high rate increase in food prices. In January 2016, annual consumer inflation took place at 9.58 per cent. On this rise, adjustments in some of the products directed and directed by food and service groups have been effective in early January. We expect inflation to fall down to 7.5 per cent in 2016, due to the continuation of the lower course of global commodity prices, the strict monetary policy, fiscal discipline, and the positive base effect from the exchange rate. It is also our goal to ensure that inflation gradually falls down and is stable in the lower single households in the mid-term. Exports were 8.7 per cent in 2015, imports narrowed by 14.4 per cent. The foreign trade deficit, which was 84.6 billion dollars in 2014, declined by 25.2 per cent in 2015 to $63.3.3 billion. Exports have increased from 65.1 per cent to 69.5 per cent. In 2015, Turkey received more from the European Union market. Cari is clearly involved in an important recession process, especially with the reduction in commodity prices, including macro-cautious measures that we've taken, and especially oil. In 2014, the rate of current revenues of 5.5 per cent was reduced to 4.5 per cent in 2015. We predict that this will decrease to 3.9% in 2016. Mr. President, our employment, which was 20.2 million people in 2007, increased to 26.7 million in November 2015. While the eurozone still has not achieved the pre-crisis level, employment in Turkey has increased by 32 per cent compared to 2007. At the same time, in recent years, workforce turnouts have been increasing, thanks to the crisis period and the policies that boosted our employment. In November 2015, the turnout to the labour force was 51.2%. In November 2015, unemployment was reduced by 0.2 points to 10.5 per cent from the same period of the previous year. We expect the unemployment rate, predicted to be 10.2% in 2015, will drop to 9.6 per cent by the end of the mid-term programme period, which is 2018. Mr. President, valued lawmakers, our strong banking system is one of the key elements that protects our economy from fluctuations. Despite the uncertainties of the global economy, the banking sector's basics maintain strong and healthy structure. As of the end of 2015, capital efficiency is about twice as high as 15.6 per cent and the legal limit is 8 per cent. The banking sector continues to maintain its active quality. The active total, one of the sector's most important indicators, grew by 18 per cent in 2015 compared to 2014 to 2.4 trillion liras. Due to the improvement of financial balances, the public sector's external debt has been reduced from 28 per cent in 2002 to 15.5 per cent in the third quarter of 2015. In private sector debt, the real-estate sector -- the external debt of companies -- increased by 13.3% in 2002 to 15.9% in the third quarter of 2015. Mr. President, the high-profile lawmakers were exposed to high budget deficits and an unsustainable burden of debt in the 1990s. Thanks to the fiscal discipline provided by our governments during the period, Turkey has been separated from the global crisis and then, especially from the developed economies in the positive direction of public finance indicators and has increased its endurance towards external shocks. Turkey has provided the Maastricht Criteria for the past 11 years, except for 2009, the global crisis year in the general state deficit. We estimate that the global state deficit, which was 10.8% in 2002, would be balanced in 2015. During 2003-2007, the overall state deficit, which averaged 2.2 per cent, averaged 0.5 per cent during 2011-2015. This performance is more dramatic in 2015, given that the overall state deficit was 4.3 per cent in developing countries, 3.3 per cent in the OECD average, and 2 per cent in the European Region. On the other hand, EU-defined debt stock, which was 74 per cent in 2002, dropped to 32.6 per cent by the end of 2015. At the end of 2018, we expect it to go below 30 percent and become 29.5%. The national rate of public debt is considerably lower than the OECD average of 115 per cent, the average of the eurozone, 93.7 per cent, and even the developing countries, which are 44.4 per cent. On the other hand, since 2002, we have reduced the rate of public net debt to 7.6 per cent from 61.5 per cent to 7.6 per cent. At the same time, we reduced the national rate of public net foreign debt from 25.2 per cent to negative 5.1 per cent, making the public sector greater. We've also extended our loans to historical high levels. By the time we reach the government, the Treasury is in debt from domestic markets at an average of 9.4 months, while it is now up to 70.4 months. Mr. President, the high-value lawmakers have achieved significant gains in our country through fiscal discipline. In 2002, 85.7% of tax revenues went to interest payments, while today only 13 per cent of tax revenues went to interest payments. Similarly, in 2002, we reduced interest costs by 43.2% in the budget to 10.5% in 2015. With all these gains, we've also made significant improvements in the costs of internal debt. At 62.7 per cent in 2002, the interest rate of fixed state interest in domestic debt bonds in terms of Turkish lira was 11.1 per cent as of January 2016. The real interest rate, which was 25.4 per cent in 2002, dropped to 3 per cent in January 2016. During our governments, we used the resources we saved by reducing interest burdens for education, health, social spending and investment spending. Our determination to maintain financial discipline has been one of the key factors that make Turkey's economy resistant to negative external shocks. In the current conjunctural, where global ambiguities are high, it is vital that these gains be taken further and further. Mr. President, valued lawmakers, through structural reforms during the EC PARTI administrations, we have achieved significant economic success through the right macroeconomic policies. Turkey's national income on a dollar basis increased 2.7 per person in 2002-2015. According to the buying power parity, the national income per person was $15,000 in 2008, while in 2015, that amount exceeded $20,000. We also healed the revenue distribution in Turkey during this period. The Gini coefficient, which measures justice in the revenue distribution, was recovered from 0.44 in 2002 to 0.38 in 2014. At the same time, the average income of the richest 10 percent of the average income of the poorest 10 per cent fell from 18.3 to 11.6 times the average income of the poorest 10 per cent. We've also made significant improvements in absolute poverty indicators. The population that lived under $2.15 per day was 3 per cent in 2002, and as of 2014, it dropped to zero. So there's no living population left with less than $2.15 in income. We reduced the population living under $4.3 a day by 28.7 points in 12 years to 1.6 per cent. Although our country has been in the middle income group for many years per capita, the average growth of 6.9 per cent during 2003-2007 has raised our country among the countries of middle-income income.
